<details>
<summary>자막: Debug web apps with browser use in Codex (1:28)</summary>

[00:00]
Codex can now better debug and test the
web applications that you're building.
We've added support for the Chrome
DevTools Protocol, or CDP, to the
Browser Use functionality in Codex.
This way, Codex has access to more
advanced functionality like performance
profiling and inspecting network
traffic, as well as the ability to
inspect console logs, runtime errors,
local storage, and the applied styling.
To use this functionality, you'll have
to enable Developer Mode in the browser
settings of your Codex app.
And since this mode gives Codex more
insight into the application it is
controlling, you'll have to explicitly
approve when Codex starts using CDP to
inspect a website.
I've been working on this little chat
app and noticed that it recently slowed
down quite significantly, especially as
the list of conversations grows.
It takes a while to load, and typing
overall is quite delayed.
I can ask Codex to debug this using
Browser Use. This way, it is not just
doing a code pass, but actually
inspecting the network traffic and
profiling the application.
This way, it can get a better
understanding of your app and the true

[00:01]
bottlenecks by profiling specific
interactions in your app or looking at
the network requests your app is making
before diving into fixing it.
You can see it found a couple of
issues, fixed them, and supported those
fixes with clear measurements showing
the improvements. This is just one
example of how Codex can use CDP to
have more in-depth Browser Use
capabilities when building applications.
Give it a try and let us know
what you think.

</details>

Codex can now work more persistently
towards your goals,
whether it's for hours or even days.

the Codex app,
IDE extension, and the CLI.
With it, you can specify a concrete goal
to achieve,
and Codex will continue to work until that
goal has been met.
Let me show you how it works.
Start by typing /goal in your message
composer to enable the goal mode.
From there, we can define our goal for
Codex.
This will act as both the original message
to have Codex start the task and as
instructions to have it determine if the
task has been completed.
The best goals are those where it is clear
whether Codex has actually achieved
the goal or not. For example, by
specifying a measurable target or having
clear test criteria that have to pass.
If you struggle writing the goal yourself,
you can ask Codex to help. Either by using
Plan Mode first before using the
resulting plan as your implementation
goal,
or by just asking Codex to interview you
before crafting the goal and then
having it set the goal itself.
Once Codex is up and running, you can
continue to use steering to send
additional messages, for example, to have
Codex correct course.

[00:01]
You can also start side chats
to understand the work done so far
without having to interrupt the main task.
Because Codex might take hours or even
days,
you can also pause and resume your goal
later on.
This is helpful, for example, when you're
about to head out of the office and
your laptop is about to lose internet
connection.
Once you're ready to continue, you can
resume the goal or even edit it if your
goal has changed or if you want to give
Codex more concrete guidance.
Using goals in Codex is incredibly
powerful.
We've seen many people have Codex make
progress on complicated tasks for
upwards of 100 hours on a single goal.

you build. For many of you, that's
Windows. So, today we're bringing the
Codex app to Windows. The Codex app lets
you work across multiple projects in one
central interface. And with the Codex
app on Windows, it runs entirely native
using PowerShell inside our new
dedicated Windows sandbox. We're also
adding all of the features people love
about the Codex app, including skills to
easily discover and extend Codex's
capabilities,
automations to have Codex work behind
the scenes for you,
work trees to work on multiple
independent tasks inside the same

Windows-specific skills in our skill
gallery, such as a dedicated WinUI skill
for all of you Windows app developers.
And for those of you who are preferring
to develop their apps using Windows
Subsystem for Linux, we've added support
to switch your coding agent and the
integrated terminal to work inside WSL.
Download the app from the Microsoft
Store or from openai.com/codex

and sign in with your existing ChatGPT
subscription or using an API key. We
can't wait to see what you build.
